## Q1

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

2,3-Dimethyl-2-butene can be prepared by heating which of the following compounds with a strong acid?

- (1) $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2C=CH-CH_2-CH_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2CH-CH_2-CH=CH_2}$
- (3) 3,4-dimethylpent-1-ene `C=CC(C)C(C)C` — $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2CH-CH(CH_3)-CH=CH_2}$
- **(4)** $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3C-CH=CH_2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: 3,3-dimethylbut-1-ene →(H⁺) 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium; 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium →(−C̄H₃) 2,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium; 2,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium →(−H⁺) 2,3-dimethylbut-2-ene (2,3-Dimethyl-2-butene)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q001_sol_1_11031cdb_28102026.png?v=11031cdb123a)

*3,3-dimethylbut-1-ene →[H⁺] 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium; 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium →[−C̄H₃] 2,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium; 2,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium →[−H⁺] 2,3-dimethylbut-2-ene (2,3-Dimethyl-2-butene)*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The target $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2C=C(CH_3)_2}$ has 6 carbons with a $\mathrm{C_4}$ chain carrying two methyls; option 3 has 7 carbons and options 1 and 2 have unbranched skeletons that cannot give it without a rearrangement of the right type.
2. Protonation of option 4 by Markovnikov addition: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3C-CH=CH_2 + H^+ \rightarrow (CH_3)_3C-\overset{+}{C}H-CH_3}$ (secondary carbocation).
3. A 1,2-methyl shift gives the more stable tertiary carbocation: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3C-\overset{+}{C}H-CH_3 \rightarrow (CH_3)_2\overset{+}{C}-CH(CH_3)_2}$.
4. Loss of $\mathrm{H^+}$ from the adjacent C–H gives the most substituted alkene: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2\overset{+}{C}-CH(CH_3)_2 \rightarrow (CH_3)_2C=C(CH_3)_2}$, i.e. 2,3-dimethyl-2-butene, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Preparation of Alkenes · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

## Q2

Chemistry · inorganic · The D- and F-Block Elements – Practice

Gadolinium belongs of 4f series. Its atomic number is 64. Which of the following is the correct electronic configuration of gadolinium?

- **(1)** $\mathrm{[Xe]4f^{7}5d^{1}6s^{2}}$
- (2) $\mathrm{[Xe]4f^{6}5d^{2}6s^{2}}$
- (3) $\mathrm{[Xe]4f^{8}6d^{2}}$
- (4) $\mathrm{[Xe]4f^{9}5s^{1}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. ${}_{64}\mathrm{Gd} = {}_{54}[\mathrm{Xe}]6s^{2}\,4f^{7}\,5d^{1}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $Z = 64 = 54\,(\mathrm{Xe}) + 10$, so $10$ electrons go beyond the $[\mathrm{Xe}]$ core.
2. $6s$ fills first: $6s^{2}$, leaving $8$ electrons.
3. A half-filled $4f^{7}$ subshell is extra stable, so $7$ go into $4f$ and the last one into $5d$: $4f^{7}5d^{1}$.
4. Configuration $[\mathrm{Xe}]4f^{7}5d^{1}6s^{2}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Electronic Configurations of the d-Block & f-Block · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 1)

![printed question 2](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q002_144e6583_28102026.png?v=144e6583df56)

## Q3

Chemistry · inorganic · Classification of Elements and Periodicity in Properties

The formation of the oxide ion, $\mathrm{O^{2-}(g)}$, from oxygen atom requires first an exothermic and then an endothermic step as shown in below:
$\mathrm{O(g) + e^{-} \longrightarrow O^{-}(g)}\;;\;\Delta_{f}H^{\ominus} = -141\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$
$\mathrm{O^{-}(g) + e^{-} \longrightarrow O^{2-}(g)}\;;\;\Delta_{f}H^{\ominus} = +780\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$
Thus process of formation of $\mathrm{O^{2-}}$ in gas phase is unfavourable though $\mathrm{O^{2-}}$ is isoelectronic with neon. It is due to the fact that,

- (1) Oxygen is more electronegative.
- (2) Addition of electron in oxygen results in larger size of the ion.
- **(3)** Electron repulsion outweighs the stability gained by achieving noble gas configuration.
- (4) $\mathrm{O^{-}}$ ion has comparatively smaller size than oxygen atom.

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Net enthalpy for $\mathrm{O(g) + 2e^{-} \rightarrow O^{2-}(g)}$: $\Delta H = -141 + 780 = +639\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, so the overall process is endothermic.
2. The second electron is added to an already negative $\mathrm{O^{-}}$ ion, so it faces strong electron-electron repulsion; this repulsion outweighs the stability gained from the neon-like $\mathrm{O^{2-}}$ configuration, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Trends in Chemical Properties – Electron Gain Enthalpy · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 1)

## Q4

Chemistry · organic · Amines

The number of structural isomers possible from the molecular formula $\mathrm{C_3H_9N}$ is:

- (1) 2
- (2) 3
- **(3)** 4
- (4) 5

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: $\ce{C3H9N}$: propan-1-amine ($\ce{CH3-CH2-CH2-NH2}$); propan-2-amine ($\ce{CH3-CH(NH2)-CH3}$) — 1° amine](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q004_sol_1_20f98730_28102026.png?v=20f987305032)

*$\ce{C3H9N}$: propan-1-amine ($\ce{CH3-CH2-CH2-NH2}$); propan-2-amine ($\ce{CH3-CH(NH2)-CH3}$) — 1° amine*

1. $\mathrm{CH_3-CH_2-NH-CH_3}$ $\}$ $2^{\circ}$ amine

![printed figure: N,N-dimethylmethanamine ($\ce{(CH3)3N}$) — 3° amine](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q004_sol_2_bddced13_28102026.png?v=bddced134af9)

*N,N-dimethylmethanamine ($\ce{(CH3)3N}$) — 3° amine*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{C_3H_9N}$ is saturated (degree of unsaturation $= \frac{2(3)+2+1-9}{2} = 0$), so only open-chain amines are possible.
2. Primary amines: $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2CH_2NH_2}$ (propan-1-amine) and $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2CHNH_2}$ (propan-2-amine), giving $2$.
3. Secondary amine: $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2NHCH_3}$ (N-methylethanamine), giving $1$.
4. Tertiary amine: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3N}$ (trimethylamine), giving $1$.
5. Total $= 2 + 1 + 1 = 4$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Structures, Nomenclature and Classification of Amines · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 1)

## Q5

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Equilibrium

If the equilibrium constant for $\mathrm{N_2(g) + O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons 2NO(g)}$ is K, the equilibrium constant for $\mathrm{\frac{1}{2}N_2(g) + \frac{1}{2}O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons NO(g)}$ will be:

- (1) $\mathrm{K}$
- (2) $\mathrm{K^{2}}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{K^{\frac{1}{2}}}$
- (4) $\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{K}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{N_2(g) + O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons 2NO(g)}$ ; $\mathrm{K}$
2. $\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{N_2(g)} + \frac{1}{2}\mathrm{O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons NO(g)}$ ; $\mathrm{K'}$
3. $\mathrm{K} = \frac{[\mathrm{NO}]^{2}}{[\mathrm{N_2}]\;[\mathrm{O_2}]}$
4. $\mathrm{K'} = \frac{\mathrm{NO}}{[\mathrm{N_2}]^{1/2}[\mathrm{O_2}]^{1/2}}$
5. $\therefore \mathrm{K'} = \sqrt{\mathrm{K}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For $\mathrm{N_2 + O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO}$, $K = \frac{[\mathrm{NO}]^2}{[\mathrm{N_2}][\mathrm{O_2}]}$.
2. For $\mathrm{\frac{1}{2}N_2 + \frac{1}{2}O_2 \rightleftharpoons NO}$, $K' = \frac{[\mathrm{NO}]}{[\mathrm{N_2}]^{1/2}[\mathrm{O_2}]^{1/2}}$.
3. $K' = \left(\frac{[\mathrm{NO}]^2}{[\mathrm{N_2}][\mathrm{O_2}]}\right)^{1/2} = K^{1/2}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Law of Chemical Equilibrium · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 5](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q005_1c25e365_28102026.png?v=1c25e365d8ff)

## Q6

Chemistry · physical · Ionic Equilibrium

Which one of the following pairs of solution is not an acidic buffer?

- (1) $\mathrm{H_2CO_3}$ and $\mathrm{Na_2CO_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{H_3PO_4}$ and $\mathrm{Na_3PO_4}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{HClO_4}$ and $\mathrm{NaClO_4}$
- (4) $\mathrm{CH_3COOH}$ and $\mathrm{CH_3COONa}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Strong acid with its salt cannot form buffer solution.
2. $\mathrm{HClO_4}$ and $\mathrm{NaClO_4}$ cannot act as an acidic buffer.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. An acidic buffer is a weak acid together with its salt with a strong base, e.g. $\mathrm{CH_3COOH}/\mathrm{CH_3COONa}$.
2. $\mathrm{H_2CO_3}$ and $\mathrm{H_3PO_4}$ are weak acids, so options $1$, $2$ and $4$ are weak acid + salt pairs.
3. $\mathrm{HClO_4}$ is a strong acid and ionises completely, so $\mathrm{HClO_4}/\mathrm{NaClO_4}$ cannot resist pH change: it is not a buffer, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Buffer Solutions · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 6](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q006_f0ce3ffb_28102026.png?v=f0ce3ffbb1d6)

## Q7

Chemistry · physical · Electrochemistry

Aqueous solution of which of the following compounds is the best conductor of electric current?

- (1) Ammonia, $\mathrm{NH_3}$
- (2) Fructose, $\mathrm{C_6H_{12}O_6}$
- (3) Acetic acid, $\mathrm{C_2H_4O_2}$
- **(4)** Hydrochloric acid, $\mathrm{HCl}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Aqueous solution of $\mathrm{HCl}$ is the best conductor of electric current because $\mathrm{HCl}$ is strong acid, so it dissociates completely into ions.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Fructose, $\mathrm{C_6H_{12}O_6}$, is a non-electrolyte and gives no ions in water.
2. $\mathrm{NH_3}$ and acetic acid $\mathrm{CH_3COOH}$ are weak electrolytes and ionise only partially.
3. $\mathrm{HCl} \rightarrow \mathrm{H^+} + \mathrm{Cl^-}$ ionises completely, so its solution has the most ions and conducts best: option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Weak Electrolytes · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

## Q8

Chemistry · organic · Polymers

Caprolactum is used for the manufacture of:

- (1) Terylene
- (2) Nylon-6,6
- **(3)** Nylon-6
- (4) Teflon

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Caprolactam is a cyclic amide, $\mathrm{C_6H_{11}NO}$, with six carbon atoms; heating it with water at high temperature opens the ring and it polymerises to $[-\mathrm{NH}-(\mathrm{CH_2})_5-\mathrm{CO}-]_n$, which is Nylon-6, option $3$.

## Q9

Chemistry · inorganic · The s-Block Elements

On heating which of the following releases $\mathrm{CO_2}$ most easily?

- **(1)** $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{CaCO_3}$
- (3) $\mathrm{K_2CO_3}$
- (4) $\mathrm{Na_2CO_3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Thermal stability order
2. $\mathrm{K_2CO_3} > \mathrm{Na_2CO_3} > \mathrm{CaCO_3} > \mathrm{MgCO_3}$
3. Therefore $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ releases $\mathrm{CO_2}$ most easily.
4. $\mathrm{MgCO_3} \xrightarrow{\Delta} \mathrm{MgO} + \mathrm{CO_2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Alkali metal carbonates ($\mathrm{Na_2CO_3}$, $\mathrm{K_2CO_3}$) are thermally very stable and do not readily lose $\mathrm{CO_2}$ on heating.
2. Group 2 carbonates decompose as $\mathrm{MCO_3 \rightarrow MO + CO_2}$, and their stability increases down the group as cation size increases and polarising power falls.
3. $\mathrm{Mg^{2+}}$ is the smallest, most polarising cation, so the order of stability is $\mathrm{MgCO_3} < \mathrm{CaCO_3} < \mathrm{Na_2CO_3} < \mathrm{K_2CO_3}$.
4. The least stable carbonate, $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$, releases $\mathrm{CO_2}$ most easily, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Alkaline Earth Metals: Properties and Trends · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 9](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q009_02f2d91e_28102026.png?v=02f2d91e678a)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 9](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q009_6a666393_28102026.png?v=6a66639315b3)

## Q10

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

Strong reducing behaviour of $\mathrm{H_3PO_2}$ is due to:

- (1) High oxidation state of phosphorus
- (2) Presence of two $-\mathrm{OH}$ groups and one p – H bond
- **(3)** Presence of one $-\mathrm{OH}$ group and two p – H bonds
- (4) High electron gain enthalpy of phosphorus

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Strong reducing behaviour of $\mathrm{H_3PO_2}$
2. All oxy-acid of phosphorus which contain $\mathrm{P-H}$ bond act as reductant.

![printed figure: phosphinic acid ($\ce{H3PO2}$): P bonded to =O, -OH and two H](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q010_sol_1_0fe7fa66_28102026.png?v=0fe7fa663dc2)

*phosphinic acid ($\ce{H3PO2}$): P bonded to =O, -OH and two H*

3. Presence of one $-\mathrm{OH}$ group and two $\mathrm{P-H}$ bonds.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{H_3PO_2}$ has the structure $\mathrm{H_2P(=O)OH}$: one $-\mathrm{OH}$ group and two $\mathrm{P{-}H}$ bonds.
2. Oxoacids of phosphorus that contain $\mathrm{P{-}H}$ bonds act as reducing agents, so the two $\mathrm{P{-}H}$ bonds account for its strong reducing behaviour, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Important Compounds and Oxyacids of Phosphorus · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 10](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q010_bdbf3422_28102026.png?v=bdbf3422d459)

## Q11

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

Decreasing order of stability of $\mathrm{O_2}$, $\mathrm{O_2^{-}}$, $\mathrm{O_2^{+}}$ and $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$ is:

- (1) $\mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}}$
- (2) $\mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$
- (4) $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{+}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Given species: $\mathrm{O_2}, \mathrm{O_2^{-}}, \mathrm{O_2^{+}}, \mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$
2. Total number of electrons
3. $\mathrm{O_2} \longrightarrow 16e^{-}$
4. $\mathrm{O_2^{-}} \longrightarrow 17e^{-}$
5. $\mathrm{O_2^{+}} \longrightarrow 15e^{-}$
6. $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}} \longrightarrow 18e^{-}$
7. Bond order: $\mathrm{O_2^{+}}$ $2.5$, $\mathrm{O_2}$ $2$, $\mathrm{O_2^{-}}$ $1.5$, $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$ $1$
8. Stability $\times$ Bond order
9. Stability order $[\mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}}]$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Stability increases with bond order, $\text{B.O.} = \frac{N_b - N_a}{2}$.
2. $\mathrm{O_2^{+}}$ (15 electrons): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-5}{2} = 2.5$.
3. $\mathrm{O_2}$ (16 electrons): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-6}{2} = 2$.
4. $\mathrm{O_2^{-}}$ (17 electrons): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-7}{2} = 1.5$.
5. $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$ (18 electrons): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-8}{2} = 1$.
6. So $\mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Molecular Orbital Theory · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed solution 11](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q011_5ef7ca89_28102026.png?v=5ef7ca891f94)

## Q12

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

The number of water molecules is maximum in:

- (1) $18\,\text{gram}$ of water
- **(2)** $18\,\text{moles}$ of water
- (3) 18 molecules of water
- (4) $1.8\,\text{gram}$ of water

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\because$ 1 mole water $= 6.02 \times 10^{23}$ moleucles
2. $\therefore$ 18 mole water $= 18 \times 6.02 \times 10^{23}$ molecules
3. So, 18 mole water has maximum number of molecules.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Molar mass of $\text{H}_2\text{O}$ is $18\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$, so $18\,\text{g} = 1\,\text{mol}$, giving $N_A = 6.022\times10^{23}$ molecules.
2. $18\,\text{mol}$ gives $18 N_A = 18 \times 6.022\times10^{23} \approx 1.08\times10^{25}$ molecules.
3. $1.8\,\text{g} = 0.1\,\text{mol}$ gives $0.1 N_A = 6.022\times10^{22}$ molecules; option 3 is only $18$ molecules.
4. The largest count is $18 N_A$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Mole Concept · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 12](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q012_9190047e_28102026.png?v=9190047e3d53)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

## Q13

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

In which of the following pairs, both the species are not isostructural?

- (1) $\mathrm{NH_3}, \mathrm{PH_3}$
- **(2)** $\mathrm{XeF_4}, \mathrm{XeO_4}$
- (3) $\mathrm{SiCl_4}, \mathrm{PCl_4^+}$
- (4) Diamond, silicon carbide

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. i. Hybridization of $\mathrm{NH_3}$ $[\sigma = 3, \mathrm{lp} = 1]$
2. $\mathrm{sp^3}$ geometry: Tetrahedral

![printed figure: ammonia, $\ce{NH3}$ (pyramidal, one lone pair on N); phosphine, $\ce{PH3}$ (pyramidal, one lone pair on P)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_1_7c82d9aa_29102026.png?v=7c82d9aa9762)

*ammonia, $\ce{NH3}$ (pyramidal, one lone pair on N); phosphine, $\ce{PH3}$ (pyramidal, one lone pair on P)*

3. ii. Structures of $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ is square planar.

![printed figure: xenon tetrafluoride, $\ce{XeF4}$, with two lone pairs on Xe (square planar), sp3 d2 hybridisation](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_2_599ae8fd_29102026.png?v=599ae8fdf6a8)

*xenon tetrafluoride, $\ce{XeF4}$, with two lone pairs on Xe (square planar), sp3 d2 hybridisation*

4. Structure of $\mathrm{XeO_4}$ is tetrahedral

![printed figure: xenon tetroxide, $\ce{XeO4}$, sp3 hybridisation](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_3_0b72ab21_29102026.png?v=0b72ab2125a7)

*xenon tetroxide, $\ce{XeO4}$, sp3 hybridisation*

5. So $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ and $\mathrm{XeO_4}$ are not isostructural.
6. iii. Structure of $\mathrm{SiCl_4}$ is tetrahedral.

![printed figure: silicon tetrachloride, $\ce{SiCl4}$, sp3 hybridisation](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_4_f629168e_29102026.png?v=f629168e9546)

*silicon tetrachloride, $\ce{SiCl4}$, sp3 hybridisation*

7. Structure of $\mathrm{PCl_4^+}$ is tetrahedral.

![printed figure: tetrachlorophosphonium ion, $\ce{PCl4+}$, sp3 hybridisation](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_5_92cc9d30_29102026.png?v=92cc9d30360c)

*tetrachlorophosphonium ion, $\ce{PCl4+}$, sp3 hybridisation*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{NH_3}$ and $\mathrm{PH_3}$ both have 3 bond pairs and 1 lone pair, so both are trigonal pyramidal: isostructural.
2. $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ has 4 bond pairs and 2 lone pairs on Xe ($sp^3d^2$), so it is square planar.
3. $\mathrm{XeO_4}$ has 4 bonding domains and no lone pair on Xe ($sp^3$), so it is tetrahedral; hence $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ and $\mathrm{XeO_4}$ are not isostructural.
4. $\mathrm{SiCl_4}$ and $\mathrm{PCl_4^+}$ both have 4 bond pairs and no lone pair ($sp^3$), so both are tetrahedral: isostructural.
5. Diamond and silicon carbide are both 3D networks of tetrahedrally bonded $sp^3$ atoms: isostructural.
6. The only non-isostructural pair is $\mathrm{XeF_4}, \mathrm{XeO_4}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion (VSEPR) Theory · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 13](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q013_c9df1834_28102026.png?v=c9df18341e8d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

## Q14

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

In the reaction with $\mathrm{HCl}$, an alkene reacts in accordance with the Markovnikov’s rule, to give a product 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane. The possible reaction alkene is:

- (1) methylenecyclohexane `C=C1CCCCC1`
- (2) 1-methylcyclohexene `CC1=CCCCC1`
- **(3)** methylenecyclohexane `C=C1CCCCC1`; 1-methylcyclohexene `CC1=CCCCC1` — and
- (4) 3-methylcyclohexene `CC1C=CCCC1`

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: methylenecyclohexane →(H⁺) 1-methylcyclohexyl cation →(Cl⁻) 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q014_sol_1_b9958495_28102026.png?v=b9958495f59a)

*methylenecyclohexane →[H⁺] 1-methylcyclohexyl cation →[Cl⁻] 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Markovnikov addition: $\mathrm{H^+}$ adds to the alkene carbon that gives the more stable (tertiary) carbocation.
2. Methylenecyclohexane ($\mathrm{C_6H_{10}{=}CH_2}$): $\mathrm{H^+}$ adds to $\mathrm{=CH_2}$, giving the tertiary cation at ring $C_1$ bearing $\mathrm{CH_3}$.
3. 1-Methylcyclohexene: $\mathrm{H^+}$ adds to $C_2$, giving the same tertiary cation at $C_1$ bearing $\mathrm{CH_3}$.
4. $\mathrm{Cl^-}$ attacks this cation to give 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane from both alkenes.
5. 3-Methylcyclohexene (option $4$) gives only secondary cations on $C_1$/$C_2$, not the 1,1-disubstituted product, so the answer is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Properties of Alkenes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 5)

![printed question 14](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q014_0d97e5d5_28102026.png?v=0d97e5d57200)

## Q15

Chemistry · physical · Redox Reactions

Assuming complete ionization, same moles of which of the following compounds will require the least amount of acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ for complete oxidation?

- (1) $\mathrm{FeC_2O_4}$
- (2) $\mathrm{Fe(NO_2)_2}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{FeSO_4}$
- (4) $\mathrm{FeSO_3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{MnO_4^{-}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Mn^{2+}}$ ; Change in oxidation no. $= 5$
2. In option,
3. i. $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$
4. Change in oxidation no. $= 1$
5. ii. $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$
6. Change in oxidation no. $= 1$
7. $\mathrm{SO_3^{2-}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{SO_4^{2-}}$
8. Change in oxidation no. $= 2$
9. $= 1 + 2 = 3$
10. iii. $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$
11. Change in oxidation no. $= 1$
12. $\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}} \longrightarrow 2\mathrm{CO_2}$
13. Change in oxidation no. $= 2$
14. $= 1 + 2 = 3$
15. iv. $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$
16. Change in oxidation no. $= 1$
17. $2\mathrm{NO_2^{-}} \longrightarrow 2\mathrm{NO_3^{-}}$
18. Change in oxidation no. $= 4$
19. $= 1 + 4 = 5$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ needed is proportional to the electrons lost per mole of reductant.
2. $\mathrm{FeC_2O_4}$: $\mathrm{Fe^{2+} \to Fe^{3+}}$ ($1e^-$) and $\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-} \to 2CO_2}$ ($2e^-$), total $3e^-$.
3. $\mathrm{Fe(NO_2)_2}$: $\mathrm{Fe^{2+} \to Fe^{3+}}$ ($1e^-$) and $2\,\mathrm{NO_2^- \to 2NO_3^-}$ ($2 \times 2 = 4e^-$), total $5e^-$.
4. $\mathrm{FeSO_4}$: only $\mathrm{Fe^{2+} \to Fe^{3+}}$ ($1e^-$); $\mathrm{SO_4^{2-}}$ is not oxidised, total $1e^-$.
5. $\mathrm{FeSO_3}$: $\mathrm{Fe^{2+} \to Fe^{3+}}$ ($1e^-$) and $\mathrm{SO_3^{2-} \to SO_4^{2-}}$ ($2e^-$), total $3e^-$.
6. Fewest electrons is $\mathrm{FeSO_4}$ ($1e^-$), so it needs the least $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** redox reactions as the basis for titrations using potassium permanganate – practice · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

## Q16

Chemistry · organic · Alcohols, Phenols and Ethers

Reaction of phenol with chloroform in presence of dilute sodium hydroxide finally introduces which one of the following functional group?

- (1) $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$
- **(2)** $-\mathrm{CHO}$
- (3) $-\mathrm{CH_2Cl}$
- (4) $-\mathrm{COOH}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Reimer Tieman reaction

![printed figure: phenol + $\ce{CHCl3}$ + $\ce{NaOH}$ → 2-hydroxybenzaldehyde > 4-hydroxybenzaldehyde](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q016_sol_1_67aef367_28102026.png?v=67aef3677b9d)

*phenol + $\ce{CHCl3}$ + $\ce{NaOH}$ → 2-hydroxybenzaldehyde > 4-hydroxybenzaldehyde*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Phenol with $\mathrm{CHCl_3}$ and dilute $\mathrm{NaOH}$ is the Reimer-Tiemann reaction; dichlorocarbene ($:\mathrm{CCl_2}$) attacks the ortho position of phenoxide, giving a $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$ intermediate.
2. In alkali the $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$ group is hydrolysed to $-\mathrm{CHO}$, so the final product is salicylaldehyde and the group introduced is $-\mathrm{CHO}$, option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Reactions of Phenols · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

## Q17

Chemistry · physical · The Solid State

The vacant space in bcc lattice unit cell is:

- (1) $23\%$
- **(2)** $32\%$
- (3) $26\%$
- (4) $48\%$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Packing efficiency in bcc lattice $= 68\%$
2. $\therefore$ Vacant space in bcc lattice $= 100 - 68 = 32\%$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A bcc unit cell holds $Z = 2$ atoms, which touch along the body diagonal: $\sqrt{3}a = 4r$, so $r = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}a$.
2. Packing fraction $= \frac{2 \times \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3}{a^3} = \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{8} \approx 0.68$, i.e. $68\%$.
3. Vacant space $= 100\% - 68\% = 32\%$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Close Packing, Tetrahedral and Octahedral Voids and Formula of Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 17](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q017_8569d87d_28102026.png?v=8569d87dd532)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 17](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q017_a01e7df0_28102026.png?v=a01e7df03b9b)

## Q18

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

Which of the statements given below is incorrect?

- (1) $\mathrm{ONF}$ is isoelectronic with $\mathrm{O_2N^-}$
- **(2)** $\mathrm{OF_2}$ is an oxide of fluorine
- (3) $\mathrm{Cl_2O_7}$ is an anhydride of perchloric acid
- (4) $\mathrm{O_3}$ molecule is bent

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. No. of electron in $\mathrm{ONF} = 24$
2. No. of electron in $\mathrm{NO_2^-} = 24$
3. Both are isoelectronic.
4. $\mathrm{OF_2}$ is a fluoride of oxygen not oxide of fluorine because EN of fluorine is more than oxygen.
5. $\mathrm{OF_2} =$ Oxygen difluoride
6. $\mathrm{Cl_2O_7}$ is an anhydride of perchloric acid.
7. $\mathrm{2HClO_4} \xrightarrow{\Delta,\,-\mathrm{H_2O}} \mathrm{Cl_2O_7}$
8. $\mathrm{O_3}$ molecules is bent shape.

![printed figure: ozone ($\ce{O3}$), bent: central O carrying a lone pair, a coordinate bond (arrow) to the left O and a double bond to the right O](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q018_sol_1_6d8f89ab_28102026.png?v=6d8f89ab9889)

*ozone ($\ce{O3}$), bent: central O carrying a lone pair, a coordinate bond (arrow) to the left O and a double bond to the right O*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Electrons in $\mathrm{ONF}$: $8 + 7 + 9 = 24$; in $\mathrm{O_2N^-}$: $2(8) + 7 + 1 = 24$, so statement 1 is correct.
2. Fluorine is more electronegative than oxygen, so in $\mathrm{OF_2}$ oxygen is the positive partner and the compound is oxygen difluoride, a fluoride of oxygen, not an oxide of fluorine; statement 2 is incorrect.
3. $\mathrm{Cl_2O_7 + H_2O \rightarrow 2HClO_4}$, so $\mathrm{Cl_2O_7}$ is the anhydride of perchloric acid; statement 3 is correct.
4. $\mathrm{O_3}$ has a lone pair on the central O and a bond angle of about $117^\circ$, so it is bent; statement 4 is correct.
5. The incorrect statement is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** General Properties & Trends of Group 17 (Halogens) · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 18](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q018_7498d5ad_28102026.png?v=7498d5ad375e)

## Q19

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

The name of complex ion, $\mathrm{[Fe(CN)_6]^{3-}}$ is :

- (1) Tricyanoferrate (III) ion
- **(2)** Hexacyanidoferrate (III) ion
- (3) Hexacyanoiron (III) ion
- (4) Hexacyanitoferrate (III) ion

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{[Fe(CN)_6]^{3-}}$
2. Hexacyanidoferrate (III) ion

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. There are six $\mathrm{CN^-}$ ligands, named $\text{cyanido}$ under IUPAC rules, so the ligand part is $\text{hexacyanido}$.
2. Oxidation state of Fe: $x + 6(-1) = -3 \Rightarrow x = +3$.
3. The complex ion is an anion, so the metal takes the $\text{-ate}$ ending with its Latin root: $\text{ferrate}$.
4. Name: $\text{hexacyanidoferrate(III) ion}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** IUPAC Nomenclature and Formulae of Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

## Q20

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

If Avogadro number $\mathrm{N_A}$ , is changed from $6.022 \times 10^{23}\,\mathrm{mol^{-1}}$ to $6.022 \times 10^{20}\,\mathrm{mol^{-1}}$, this would change:

- (1) The ratio of chemical species to each other in a balanced equation.
- (2) The ratio of elements to each other in a compound.
- (3) The definition of mass in units of grams
- **(4)** The mass of one mole of carbon

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\because$ Mass of $1$ mol ($6.022 \times 10^{23}$ atoms) of carbon $= 12\,\mathrm{g}$
2. If Avogadro Number ($\mathrm{N_A}$) is changed then mass of $1$ mol ($6.022 \times 10^{20}$ atom) of carbon
3. $= \frac{12 \times 6.022 \times 10^{20}}{6.022 \times 10^{23}} = 12 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{g}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Ratios of species in a balanced equation and of elements in a compound are ratios of numbers of particles, so they do not depend on $N_A$; options $1$ and $2$ are unchanged.
2. The gram is a unit of mass defined independently of $N_A$, so option $3$ is unchanged.
3. Mass of one mole of carbon $= N_A \times m_{\mathrm{C\,atom}} = 6.022 \times 10^{20} \times 1.993 \times 10^{-23}\,\mathrm{g} \approx 0.012\,\mathrm{g}$, which differs from $12\,\mathrm{g}$.
4. Only the mass of one mole of carbon changes, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Mole Concept · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 20](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q020_1d3519be_28102026.png?v=1d3519be2ab5)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 20](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q020_c4d99e2e_28102026.png?v=c4d99e2e1bc7)

## Q21

Chemistry · organic · General Organic Chemistry

Which of the following statements is not correct for a nucleophile?

- (1) Nucleophiles attack low $\mathrm{e^{-}}$ density sites
- (2) Nucleophiles are not electron seeking
- **(3)** Nucleophile is a Lewis acid
- (4) Ammonia is a nucleophile

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Reason: Nucleophiles are electron rich species so act as Lewis base.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A nucleophile ($\mathrm{Nu^{:}}$) is nucleus-seeking: it donates an electron pair and attacks electron-deficient (low $\mathrm{e^{-}}$ density) sites, so statements $1$ and $2$ are correct.
2. $\mathrm{NH_3}$ has a lone pair on N, so ammonia is a nucleophile; statement $4$ is correct.
3. An electron-pair donor is a Lewis base, not a Lewis acid, so statement $3$ is not correct, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Electrophile and Nucleophile · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

## Q22

Chemistry · physical · States of Matter

A gas such as carbon monoxide would be most likely to obey the ideal gas law at:

- (1) High temperatures and high pressures
- (2) Low temperatures and low pressures
- **(3)** High temperatures and low pressures
- (4) Low temperatures and high pressures

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Real gases show ideal gas behaviour at high temperatures and low pressures.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Ideal behaviour needs negligible molecular volume and negligible intermolecular attraction, i.e. compressibility factor $Z = \frac{PV}{nRT} \to 1$.
2. At low pressure the gas volume is large, so the volume of the molecules themselves is negligible.
3. At high temperature the kinetic energy is large, so intermolecular attractions are negligible; hence high $T$ and low $P$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Real Gases and van der Waals Equation · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 22](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q022_c3f4ef47_28102026.png?v=c3f4ef472556)

## Q23

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

The hybridization involved in complex $\mathrm{[Ni(CN)_4]^{2-}}$ is: (Atomic number of Ni = $28$)

- (1) $\mathrm{d^2sp^2}$
- (2) $\mathrm{d^2sp^3}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{dsp^2}$
- (4) $\mathrm{sp^3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{[Ni(CN)_4]^{2-}}$
2. Oxidation state of $\mathrm{Ni}$ is $+2$
3. $x - 4 = 2$
4. $x = +2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Oxidation state of Ni: $x + 4(-1) = -2 \Rightarrow x = +2$.
2. $\mathrm{Ni}$ ($Z=28$): $[\mathrm{Ar}]\,3d^8 4s^2$, so $\mathrm{Ni^{2+}}$: $[\mathrm{Ar}]\,3d^8$.
3. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is a strong-field ligand, so the eight $3d$ electrons pair up in four $3d$ orbitals, leaving one $3d$ orbital empty.
4. Four ligands use one $3d$, one $4s$ and two $4p$ orbitals: $\mathrm{dsp^2}$ hybridization, square planar, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Bond Theory, Its Limitations and Magnetic Properties of Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 23](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q023_c71fc951_28102026.png?v=c71fc951f849)

## Q24

Chemistry · physical · Thermodynamics

The heat of combustion of carbon to $\mathrm{CO_2}$ is $-393.5\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}$. The heat released upon formation of $35.2\,\mathrm{g}$ of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ from carbon and oxygen gas is:

- (1) $-630\,\mathrm{kJ}$
- (2) $-3.15\,\mathrm{kJ}$
- **(3)** $-315\,\mathrm{kJ}$
- (4) $+315\,\mathrm{kJ}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Formation of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ from carbon and dioxygen gas can be represented as
2. $\mathrm{C(s)} + \mathrm{O_{2(g)}} \rightarrow \mathrm{CO_{2(g)}}\,;\ \Delta_f H = -393.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$
3. ($1\,\mathrm{mole} = 44\,\mathrm{g}$)
4. Heat released on formation of $44\,\mathrm{g}$ $\mathrm{CO_2}$
5. $= -393.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$
6. $= \frac{-393.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}}{44\,\mathrm{g}} \times 35.2\,\mathrm{g}$
7. $= -315\,\mathrm{kJ}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Molar mass of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ is $12 + 2\times16 = 44\,\mathrm{g/mol}$.
2. $n = \frac{35.2\,\mathrm{g}}{44\,\mathrm{g/mol}} = 0.8\,\mathrm{mol}$.
3. $\Delta H = 0.8 \times (-393.5\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}) = -314.8\,\mathrm{kJ} \approx -315\,\mathrm{kJ}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Thermochemical Equations · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

## Q25

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

$20.0\,\mathrm{g}$ of a magnesium carbonate sample decomposes on heating to give carbon dioxide and $8.0\,\mathrm{g}$ magnesium oxide. What will be the percentage purity of magnesium carbonate in the sample? (Atomic weight: $\mathrm{Mg} = 24$)

- (1) 60
- **(2)** 84
- (3) 75
- (4) 96

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{MgCO_3(s)} \longrightarrow \mathrm{MgO(s)} + \mathrm{CO_2(g)}$
2. Moles of $\mathrm{MgCO_3} = \frac{20}{84} = 0.238\,\mathrm{mol}$
3. From above equation.
4. $1$ mole $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ gives $1$ mole $\mathrm{MgO}$
5. $\therefore$ $0.238$ mole $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ will give $0.238$ mole $\mathrm{MgO}$
6. $= 0.238 \times 40\,\mathrm{g} = 9.523\,\mathrm{g}\ \mathrm{MgO}$
7. Practical yield of $\mathrm{MgO} = 8\,\mathrm{g}\ \mathrm{MgO}$
8. $\therefore \%\ \text{Purity} = \frac{8}{9.523} \times 100 = 84\%$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{MgCO_3 \rightarrow MgO + CO_2}$, so 1 mol $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ gives 1 mol $\mathrm{MgO}$.
2. $M(\mathrm{MgO}) = 24 + 16 = 40\,\mathrm{g\,mol^{-1}}$ and $M(\mathrm{MgCO_3}) = 24 + 12 + 3(16) = 84\,\mathrm{g\,mol^{-1}}$.
3. $n(\mathrm{MgO}) = \frac{8.0}{40} = 0.2\,\mathrm{mol}$.
4. $m(\mathrm{MgCO_3}) = 0.2 \times 84 = 16.8\,\mathrm{g}$.
5. Purity $= \frac{16.8}{20.0} \times 100 = 84\%$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Stoichiometry · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 25](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q025_89a8f4e1_28102026.png?v=89a8f4e1536d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 25](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q025_1bb29c7b_28102026.png?v=1bb29c7bd7e5)

## Q26

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

What is the mole fraction of the solute in a $1.00\,\mathrm{m}$ aqueous solution?

- (1) 0.0354
- **(2)** 0.0177
- (3) 0.177
- (4) 1.770

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $1.0\,\mathrm{m}$ solution means $1$ mole solute is present in $1000\,\mathrm{g}$ water.
2. $n_{\mathrm{H_2O}} = 55.5\,\mathrm{mol}\ \mathrm{H_2O}$
3. $X_{\mathrm{Solute}} = \frac{n_{\mathrm{Solute}}}{n_{\mathrm{Solute}} + n_{\mathrm{H_2O}}} = \frac{1}{1 + 55.5} = 0.0177$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A $1.00\,\mathrm{m}$ solution has $1\,\mathrm{mol}$ of solute in $1000\,\mathrm{g}$ of water.
2. $n_{\mathrm{H_2O}} = \frac{1000}{18} = 55.51\,\mathrm{mol}$.
3. $x_{\text{solute}} = \frac{1}{1 + 55.51} = \frac{1}{56.51} = 0.0177$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Mole Fraction · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

## Q27

Chemistry · physical · The Solid State

The correct statement regarding defects in crystalline solids is :

- **(1)** Frenkel defect is a dislocation defect
- (2) Frenkel defect is found in halides of alkaline metals
- (3) Schottky defects have no effect on the density of crystalline solids
- (4) Frenkel defects decrease the density of crystalline solids

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Frenkel defect is a dislocation defect.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A Frenkel defect moves an ion from its lattice site to an interstitial site, so it is called a dislocation defect (option $1$ true); alkali metal halides show Schottky defects, not Frenkel (option $2$ false); Schottky defects lower density (option $3$ false); Frenkel defects leave density unchanged (option $4$ false), so the answer is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Imperfections in Solids · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

## Q28

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

The stability of $+1$ oxidation state among Al, Ga, In and TI increases in the sequence:

- (1) $\mathrm{TI} < \mathrm{In} < \mathrm{Ga} < \mathrm{Al}$
- (2) $\mathrm{In} < \mathrm{TI} < \mathrm{Ga} < \mathrm{Al}$
- (3) $\mathrm{Ga} < \mathrm{In} < \mathrm{Al} < \mathrm{TI}$
- **(4)** $\mathrm{Al} < \mathrm{Ga} < \mathrm{In} < \mathrm{TI}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Stability of $+1$ oxidation state due to inert pair effect $\mathrm{TI} < \mathrm{In} < \mathrm{Ga} < \mathrm{Al}$.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Down group 13, the inert pair effect strengthens, so the $ns^2$ pair is less available for bonding and the $+1$ state becomes more stable: $\mathrm{Al} < \mathrm{Ga} < \mathrm{In} < \mathrm{Tl}$, which is option $4$.

## Q29

Chemistry · organic · Isomerism in Organic Compounds

Two possible stereo-structures of $\mathrm{CH_3CHOH \cdot COOH}$, which are optically active, are called:

- **(1)** Enantiomers
- (2) Mesomers
- (3) Diastereomers
- (4) Atropisomers

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Fischer projections on either side of a mirror: (2R)-2-hydroxypropanoic acid (COOH top, CH3 bottom, H left, OH right) | mirror | (2S)-2-hydroxypropanoic acid (COOH top, CH3 bottom, HO left, H right)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q029_sol_1_995cca56_28102026.png?v=995cca563063)

1. Both are enantiomers.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{CH_3CH(OH)COOH}$ (lactic acid) has one chiral carbon bearing $\mathrm{H}$, $\mathrm{OH}$, $\mathrm{CH_3}$ and $\mathrm{COOH}$.
2. With one stereocentre there are $2^1 = 2$ stereoisomers, the $(+)$ and $(-)$ forms, which are non-superimposable mirror images.
3. Non-superimposable mirror-image stereoisomers are enantiomers, which is option $1$.

## Q31

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

The sum of coordination number and oxidation number of metal M in the complex $\mathrm{[M(en)_2(C_2O_4)]Cl}$ (Where en is ethylenediamine) is:

- (1) 7
- (2) 8
- **(3)** 9
- (4) 6

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{[M(en)_2(C_2O_4)]Cl}$
2. Oxidation state of $\mathrm{M} = +3$
3. Coordination number of $\mathrm{M} = 6$
4. Sum of oxidation state + Coordination number $= 3 + 6 = 9$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Each $\mathrm{en}$ is a neutral bidentate ligand and $\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}}$ is a bidentate ligand with charge $-2$.
2. Coordination number $= 2 \times 2 + 1 \times 2 = 6$.
3. The complex ion is $\mathrm{[M(en)_2(C_2O_4)]^{+}}$ because one $\mathrm{Cl^-}$ is the counter ion.
4. $x + 2(0) + (-2) = +1 \Rightarrow x = +3$.
5. $6 + 3 = 9$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Coordination and Oxidation Number of Central Atom · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 11)

![printed question 31](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q031_def9086e_28102026.png?v=def9086ea2de)

## Q32

Chemistry · organic · Aldehydes, Ketones, and Carboxylic Acids

Reaction of a carbonyl compound with one of the following reagents involves nucleophilic addition followed by elimination of water. The reagent is:

- (1) Hydrocyanic acid
- (2) Sodium hydrogen sulphite
- (3) A Grignard reagent
- **(4)** Hydrazine in presence of feebly acidic solution

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. With Ammonia derivation carbonyl compounds give addition followed by elimination reaction.
2. Slightly acidic medium will generate a nucleophilic centre for weak base like ammonia derivatives.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. HCN, $\mathrm{NaHSO_3}$ and a Grignard reagent give simple nucleophilic addition products (cyanohydrin, bisulphite adduct, alkoxide/alcohol) with no loss of water.
2. In feebly acidic medium hydrazine adds to the carbonyl carbon: $\mathrm{R_2C{=}O + H_2N{-}NH_2 \rightarrow R_2C(OH){-}NH{-}NH_2}$.
3. The carbinolamine then eliminates water: $\mathrm{R_2C(OH){-}NH{-}NH_2 \rightarrow R_2C{=}N{-}NH_2 + H_2O}$ (hydrazone), so the answer is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Nucleophilic Addition Reactions · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

## Q33

Chemistry · organic · Aldehydes, Ketones, and Carboxylic Acids

Which one of the following esters gets hydrolyzed most easily under alkaline conditions?

- (1) phenyl acetate `CC(=O)Oc1ccccc1`
- (2) 4-chlorophenyl acetate `CC(=O)Oc1ccc(Cl)cc1`
- **(3)** 4-nitrophenyl acetate `CC(=O)Oc1ccc([N+](=O)[O-])cc1`
- (4) 4-methoxyphenyl acetate `COc1ccc(OC(C)=O)cc1`

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. EWG (Electron withdrawing group) increases reactivity towards nucleophilic substitution reaction.
2. $-\mathrm{NO_2}$ is strong electron withdrawing group.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Alkaline hydrolysis of an ester begins when $\mathrm{OH^-}$ attacks the carbonyl carbon, then the aryloxide $\mathrm{ArO^-}$ leaves.
2. Electron-withdrawing groups on the ring make the carbonyl carbon more electrophilic and stabilise $\mathrm{ArO^-}$, so they speed up hydrolysis.
3. $\mathrm{-NO_2}$ (strong $-I$, $-M$) > $\mathrm{-Cl}$ (weak net $-I$) > $\mathrm{-H}$ > $\mathrm{-OCH_3}$ ($+M$, electron-donating).
4. So the para-$\mathrm{NO_2}$ ester, option $3$, is hydrolysed most easily.

**Subtopic:** Substitution Reactions · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 11)

![printed question 33](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q033_715f5449_28102026.png?v=715f544972e8)

![printed solution 33](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q033_30a3e0f8_28102026.png?v=30a3e0f810a3)

## Q34

Chemistry · organic · Haloalkanes and Haloarenes

In an $\mathrm{S_N1}$ reaction on chiral centres, there is:

- (1) $100\%$ retention
- (2) $100\%$ inversion
- (3) $100\%$ racemization
- **(4)** Inversion more than retention leading to partial racemization

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{S_N1}$ reaction gives racemic mixture with slight predominance of that isomer which corresponds to inversion because $\mathrm{S_N1}$ also depends upon the degree of 'shielding' of the front side of the reacting carbon.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In an $\mathrm{S_N1}$ reaction the rate-determining step is ionisation, giving a planar $sp^2$ carbocation $\mathrm{R^+}$.
2. The nucleophile can attack the planar carbocation from either face, giving both retention and inversion products, so racemization occurs.
3. The leaving group $\mathrm{X^-}$ stays close as an ion pair and partly blocks the front face, so backside attack (inversion) is somewhat favoured.
4. Hence inversion is more than retention, giving partial racemization, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Stereochemical Aspect of Nucleophilic Substitution · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 12)

![printed question 34](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q034_d473af0c_28102026.png?v=d473af0c4f80)

## Q35

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Kinetics

The rate constant of the reaction $\mathrm{A} \longrightarrow \mathrm{B}$ is $0.6 \times 10^{-3}$ mole per second. If the concentration of A is $5\,\mathrm{M}$, then concentration of B after 20 minutes is:

- (1) $0.36\,\mathrm{M}$
- **(2)** $0.72\,\mathrm{M}$
- (3) $1.08\,\mathrm{M}$
- (4) $3.60\,\mathrm{M}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. For zero order reaction:
2. $x = K \cdot t$
3. $= 0.6 \times 10^{-3} \times 20 \times 60$
4. $x = 0.72\,\mathrm{M}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The units of $k$ are concentration per time ($\mathrm{mol\,L^{-1}\,s^{-1}}$), so the reaction is zero order.
2. For zero order, $[\mathrm{A}]_0 - [\mathrm{A}] = kt$, so $[\mathrm{B}] = kt$.
3. $t = 20\,\mathrm{min} = 20 \times 60 = 1200\,\mathrm{s}$.
4. $[\mathrm{B}] = 0.6 \times 10^{-3} \times 1200 = 0.72\,\mathrm{M}$, which is less than $5\,\mathrm{M}$, so this is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Zero Order Reactions · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 12)

![printed question 35](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q035_c4ec29ae_28102026.png?v=c4ec29ae33f7)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 35](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q035_0e30fa03_28102026.png?v=0e30fa035a07)

## Q36

Chemistry · physical · Ionic Equilibrium

What is the pH of the resulting solution when equal volumes of $0.1\,\mathrm{m}$ $\mathrm{NaOH}$ and $0.01\,\mathrm{M}$ $\mathrm{HCl}$ are mixed?

- (1) 7.0
- (2) 1.04
- **(3)** 12.65
- (4) 2.0

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{N_1V_1} - \mathrm{N_2V_2} = \mathrm{N.V.}$
2. $0.1 \times 1 - 0.01 \times 1 = \mathrm{N} \times 2$
3. $[\mathrm{OH^-}] = \mathrm{N_R} = 0.\frac{09}{2} = 0.045\,\mathrm{N}$
4. $\mathrm{pOH} = -\log(0.045) = 1.35$
5. $\therefore \mathrm{pH} = 14 - \mathrm{pOH} = 14 - 1.35 = 12.65$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Equal volumes $V$: moles of $\mathrm{OH^-} = 0.1V$, moles of $\mathrm{H^+} = 0.01V$.
2. Neutralisation leaves excess $\mathrm{OH^-} = 0.1V - 0.01V = 0.09V$.
3. Total volume is $2V$, so $[\mathrm{OH^-}] = \frac{0.09V}{2V} = 0.045\,\mathrm{M}$.
4. $\mathrm{pOH} = -\log(0.045) = 2 - \log 4.5 \approx 2 - 0.653 = 1.35$.
5. $\mathrm{pH} = 14 - 1.35 = 12.65$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** pH Scale · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 12)

![printed question 36](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q036_48fc95ae_28102026.png?v=48fc95ae3fe1)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 36](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q036_397b69cb_28102026.png?v=397b69cbd30a)

## Q37

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

Number of possible isomers for the complex $\mathrm{[Co(en)_2Cl_2]Cl}$ will be: (en = ethylene diamine)

- **(1)** $3$
- (2) $4$
- (3) $2$
- (4) $1$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{[Co(en)_2Cl_2]Cl}$
2. Possible isomers
3. (i) Geometrical isomers

![printed figure: octahedral geometry diagram of cis-$\ce{(Co(en)2Cl2)+}$: Co at the centre, two Cl adjacent (cis), two en chelate rings drawn as arcs; labelled cis → optically active](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q037_sol_1_cf4df569_28102026.png?v=cf4df56907ea)

*octahedral geometry diagram of cis-$\ce{[Co(en)2Cl2]+}$: Co at the centre, two Cl adjacent (cis), two en chelate rings drawn as arcs; labelled cis → optically active*


![printed figure: octahedral geometry diagram of trans-$\ce{(Co(en)2Cl2)+}$: Co at the centre, two Cl opposite each other (axial, trans), two en chelate rings drawn as arcs on either side; labelled trans → optically inactive](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q037_sol_2_223f393c_28102026.png?v=223f393cbfa2)

*octahedral geometry diagram of trans-$\ce{[Co(en)2Cl2]+}$: Co at the centre, two Cl opposite each other (axial, trans), two en chelate rings drawn as arcs on either side; labelled trans → optically inactive*

4. (ii) In trans form plane of symmetry present, so trans form is optically inactive but cis is optically active.
5. Total number of stereoisomer $= 2 + 1 = 3$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The complex ion $\mathrm{[Co(en)_2Cl_2]^+}$ is octahedral of type $\mathrm{M(AA)_2B_2}$.
2. It shows geometric isomerism: $cis$ and $trans$ forms.
3. The $trans$ form has a plane of symmetry, so it is optically inactive: $1$ isomer.
4. The $cis$ form has no plane of symmetry, so it exists as $d$ and $l$ enantiomers: $2$ isomers.
5. Total $= 1 + 2 = 3$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Isomerism in Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 12)

## Q38

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

The variation of the boiling points of the hydrogen halides is in the order $\mathrm{HF} > \mathrm{HI} > \mathrm{HBr} > \mathrm{HCl}$. What explains the higher boiling point of hydrogen fluoride?

- (1) The bond energy of $\mathrm{HF}$ molecules is greater than in other hydrogen halides.
- (2) The effect of nuclear shielding is much reduced in fluorine which polarizes the $\mathrm{HF}$ molecule.
- (3) The electronegativity of fluorine is much higher than for other elements in the group.
- **(4)** There is strong hydrogen bonding between $\mathrm{HF}$ molecules.

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Due to strong H-bonding in $\mathrm{HF}$ molecule, boiling point is highest for $\mathrm{HF}$.
2. $\mathrm{HF} > \mathrm{HI} > \mathrm{HBr} > \mathrm{HCl}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Boiling depends on intermolecular forces, not on the intramolecular $\mathrm{H-F}$ bond energy, so strong intermolecular hydrogen bonding ($\mathrm{H-F\cdots H-F}$) explains the anomalously high boiling point of $\mathrm{HF}$, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Hydrogen Bonding · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 13)

![printed question 38](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q038_731efe2f_28102026.png?v=731efe2f32fd)

![printed solution 38](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q038_6a5dffd1_28102026.png?v=6a5dffd17051)

## Q39

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

What is the mass of the precipitate formed when $50\,\mathrm{mL}$ of $16.9\%$ solution of $\mathrm{AgNO_3}$ is mixed with $50\,\mathrm{mL}$ of $5.8\%$ $\mathrm{NaCl}$ solution?

- **(1)** $7\,\mathrm{g}$
- (2) $14\,\mathrm{g}$
- (3) $28\,\mathrm{g}$
- (4) $3.5\,\mathrm{g}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $16.9\,\mathrm{g}$ $\mathrm{AgNO_3}$ is present in $100\,\mathrm{mL}$ solution.
2. $\therefore$ $8.45\,\mathrm{g}$ $\mathrm{AgNo_3}$ is present in $50\,\mathrm{mL}$ solution
3. $5.8\,\mathrm{g}$ $\mathrm{NaCl}$ is present in $100\,\mathrm{mL}$ solution
4. $\therefore$ $2.9\,\mathrm{g}$ $\mathrm{NaCl}$ is present in $50\,\mathrm{mL}$ solution
5. $\mathrm{AgNO_3} + \mathrm{NaCl} \rightarrow \mathrm{AgCl} + \mathrm{NaNO_3}$
6. $\frac{8.45}{170}\,\mathrm{mol} \qquad \frac{2.9}{58.5}$
7. $= 0.049\,\mathrm{mol} \qquad = 0.049\,\mathrm{mol} \rightarrow 0 \qquad 0$
8. After reaction $\qquad 0 \qquad 0 \rightarrow 0.049\,\mathrm{mol} \qquad 0.049\,\mathrm{mol}$
9. Mass of $\mathrm{AgCl}$ precipitated
10. $= 0.049 \times 143.5\,\mathrm{g}$
11. $= 7\,\mathrm{g}$ $\mathrm{AgCl}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Mass of $\mathrm{AgNO_3}$ in $50\,\mathrm{mL}$ of a $16.9\%$ (w/v) solution: $\frac{16.9}{100} \times 50 = 8.45\,\mathrm{g}$.
2. Moles of $\mathrm{AgNO_3}$: $\frac{8.45}{170} \approx 0.05\,\mathrm{mol}$.
3. Mass of $\mathrm{NaCl}$ in $50\,\mathrm{mL}$ of a $5.8\%$ (w/v) solution: $\frac{5.8}{100} \times 50 = 2.9\,\mathrm{g}$.
4. Moles of $\mathrm{NaCl}$: $\frac{2.9}{58.5} \approx 0.05\,\mathrm{mol}$.
5. $\mathrm{AgNO_3 + NaCl \rightarrow AgCl\downarrow + NaNO_3}$ is $1:1$, so about $0.05\,\mathrm{mol}$ of $\mathrm{AgCl}$ precipitates.
6. Mass of $\mathrm{AgCl}$: $0.05 \times 143.5 = 7.175\,\mathrm{g} \approx 7\,\mathrm{g}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Stoichiometry · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 13)

![printed question 39](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q039_ca177bf1_28102026.png?v=ca177bf19ac8)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 39](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q039_15a24811_28102026.png?v=15a24811d591)

## Q40

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

The oxidation of benzene by $\mathrm{V_2O_5}$ in the presence of air produces:

- (1) Benzoic acid
- (2) Benzaldehyde
- (3) Benzoic anhydride
- **(4)** Maleic anhydride

![printed figure: benzene →(Air / $\ce{V2O5}$) maleic anhydride](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q040_sol_1_7d16418a_28102026.png?v=7d16418aecb9)

*benzene →[Air / $\ce{V2O5}$] maleic anhydride*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Air oxidation of benzene over $\mathrm{V_2O_5}$ at about $773\,\mathrm{K}$ opens the ring and gives maleic anhydride: $\mathrm{C_6H_6} + \tfrac{9}{2}\mathrm{O_2} \xrightarrow{\mathrm{V_2O_5},\ 773\,\mathrm{K}} \mathrm{C_4H_2O_3} + 2\mathrm{CO_2} + 2\mathrm{H_2O}$, which is option $4$.

## Q41

Chemistry · organic · Alcohols, Phenols and Ethers

> ⚠ The independent solve chose (3); the booklet prints (4).

Which of the following is not the product of dehydration of [structure] ?

![printed figure: 2-cyclohexylbutan-2-ol](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q041_3e805f16_28102026.png?v=3e805f164d00)

*2-cyclohexylbutan-2-ol*

Structures: 2-cyclohexylbutan-2-ol `CCC(C)(O)C1CCCCC1`

- (1) 2-cyclohexylbut-2-ene `CC=C(C)C1CCCCC1`
- (2) butan-2-ylidenecyclohexane `CCC(C)=C1CCCCC1`
- (3) 2-cyclohexylbut-1-ene `C=C(CC)C1CCCCC1`
- **(4)** 1-(butan-2-yl)cyclohex-1-ene `CCC(C)C1=CCCCC1`

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (3) · differs

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: 2-cyclohexylbutan-2-ol →(Δ, -$\ce{H2O}$) butan-2-ylidenecyclohexane + 2-cyclohexylbut-1-ene + 2-cyclohexylbut-2-ene](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q041_sol_1_ffcd149d_28102026.png?v=ffcd149d9fef)

*2-cyclohexylbutan-2-ol →[Δ, -$\ce{H2O}$] butan-2-ylidenecyclohexane + 2-cyclohexylbut-1-ene + 2-cyclohexylbut-2-ene*

1. Intermediate carbocation (more stable). No rearangement in $\mathrm{C^{+}}$ takes place.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Protonation of $-OH$ and loss of $H_2O$ gives a tertiary carbocation on the ring carbon $C_1$ that also carries the $CH(CH_3)CH_2CH_3$ group.
2. Loss of a ring $\beta$-H from this cation gives the endocyclic alkene, option $4$.
3. Loss of the side-chain methine $\beta$-H gives the exocyclic tetrasubstituted alkene $C_6H_{10}{=}C(CH_3)CH_2CH_3$, option $2$.
4. Options $1$ and $3$ need a $1,2$-hydride shift to the side-chain tertiary cation $C_6H_{11}\text{-}C^+(CH_3)CH_2CH_3$.
5. From that cation, loss of $H$ from $CH_2$ gives the trisubstituted alkene, option $1$; loss of $H$ from $CH_3$ gives the disubstituted terminal alkene $C_6H_{11}C(=CH_2)CH_2CH_3$, option $3$, the least stable and least favoured.
6. Best choice for the product not formed: option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Reactions of Alcohols · **Difficulty:** unsure (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 14)

![printed question 41](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q041_ac7e79ad_28102026.png?v=ac7e79ad4bea)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 41](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q041_4ba43c2a_28102026.png?v=4ba43c2a350c)

## Q42

Chemistry · organic · Amines

Method by which Aniline cannot be prepared is:

- (1) Reduction of nitrobenzene with $\mathrm{H_2/Pd}$ in ethanol.
- **(2)** Potassium salt of phthalimide treated with chlorobenzene followed by hydrolysis with aqueous $\mathrm{NaOH}$ solution.
- (3) Hydrolysis of phenylisocyanide with acidic solution
- (4) Degradation of benzamide with bromine in alkaline solution

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: chlorobenzene, with lone pairs drawn on Cl and a curved arrow from a Cl lone pair to the ring bond (resonance)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q042_sol_1_26f9aac9_28102026.png?v=26f9aac90ae6)

*chlorobenzene, with lone pairs drawn on Cl and a curved arrow from a Cl lone pair to the ring bond (resonance)*

1. Due to resonance $\mathrm{C-Cl}$ bond acquires double bond character.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Options 1, 3 and 4 all give aniline: $\mathrm{C_6H_5NO_2 \xrightarrow{H_2/Pd} C_6H_5NH_2}$; $\mathrm{C_6H_5NC + 2H_2O \xrightarrow{H^+} C_6H_5NH_2 + HCOOH}$; $\mathrm{C_6H_5CONH_2 \xrightarrow{Br_2/NaOH} C_6H_5NH_2}$ (Hofmann bromamide degradation).
2. In the Gabriel synthesis, the phthalimide anion cannot displace $\mathrm{Cl}$ from chlorobenzene (aryl halides resist nucleophilic substitution), so aniline cannot be prepared this way, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Methods of Preparation of Amines · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 14)

![printed question 42](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q042_c046012a_28102026.png?v=c046012a9a87)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 42](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q042_555fda20_28102026.png?v=555fda207316)

## Q43

Chemistry · organic · Haloalkanes and Haloarenes

Which of the following reaction(s) can be used for the preparation of alkyl halides?

- **(i.)** $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH + HCl \xrightarrow{Anhyd.ZnCl_2}}$
- **(ii.)** $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH + HCl \longrightarrow}$
- **(iii.)** $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3COH + HCl \longrightarrow}$
- **(iv.)** $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2CHOH + HCl \xrightarrow{Anhyd.ZnCl_2}}$

- (1) IV only
- (2) III and IV only
- **(3)** I, III and IV only
- (4) I and II only

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. I and IV can be used due to presence of anhydrous $\mathrm{ZnCl_2}$
2. (III) gives alkyl halide due to formation of more stable carbocation.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Reactivity of alcohols with $\mathrm{HCl}$ follows $3^\circ > 2^\circ > 1^\circ$; $1^\circ$ and $2^\circ$ alcohols need anhydrous $\mathrm{ZnCl_2}$ as catalyst (Groves process).
2. I: $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH + HCl \xrightarrow{Anhyd.\,ZnCl_2} CH_3CH_2Cl}$, so I works.
3. II: $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2OH + HCl}$ without $\mathrm{ZnCl_2}$ does not give the alkyl chloride effectively, so II is rejected.
4. III: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3COH + HCl \longrightarrow (CH_3)_3CCl}$ readily via a stable $3^\circ$ carbocation, so III works.
5. IV: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2CHOH + HCl \xrightarrow{Anhyd.\,ZnCl_2} (CH_3)_2CHCl}$, so IV works.
6. I, III and IV only is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Methods of Preparation of Haloalkanes from Alcohols, Halogen Exchange, and Hydrocarbons · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 15)

![printed question 43](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q043_a246f537_28102026.png?v=a246f5375eae)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 43](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q043_065dd951_28102026.png?v=065dd951f18c)

## Q44

Chemistry · physical · Structure of Atom

Which is the correct order of increasing energy of the listed orbitals in the atom of titanium? (At. No. $Z = 22$)

- (1) 3s 3p 3d 4s
- **(2)** 3s 3p 4s 3d
- (3) 3s 4s 3p 3d
- (4) 4s 3s 3p 3d

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{Ti}(22) = 1s^{2}2s^{2}2p^{6}3s^{2}3p^{6}4s^{2}3d^{2}$
2. Order of energy is $3s$ $3p$ $4s$ $3d$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Apply the $(n+l)$ rule: $3s: 3+0=3$, $3p: 3+1=4$, $4s: 4+0=4$, $3d: 3+2=5$.
2. For equal $(n+l)$, the orbital with lower $n$ has lower energy, so $3p < 4s$.
3. Order of increasing energy: $3s < 3p < 4s < 3d$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Atomic Orbitals: Shapes, Energies and Nodes · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 15)

![printed question 44](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q044_e25d6dcb_28102026.png?v=e25d6dcb0cf2)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 44](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q044_3bf63f9e_28102026.png?v=3bf63f9eb8f0)

## Q45

Chemistry · inorganic · General Principles and Processes of Isolation of Elements

> ⚠ The independent solve chose (1); the booklet prints (2).

In the extraction of copper from its sulphide ore, the metal is finally obtained by the reduction of cuprous oxide with:

- (1) Copper (I) sulphide
- **(2)** Sulphur dioxide
- (3) Iron (II) sulphide
- (4) Carbon monoxide

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (1) · differs

**Publisher's solution**

1. Self reduction
2. $\mathrm{Cu_2S} + 2\mathrm{Cu_2O} \longrightarrow 6\mathrm{Cu} + \mathrm{SO_2}\uparrow$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In the converter, part of the matte is oxidised: $2\mathrm{Cu_2S} + 3\mathrm{O_2} \rightarrow 2\mathrm{Cu_2O} + 2\mathrm{SO_2}$.
2. The remaining $\mathrm{Cu_2S}$ reduces the cuprous oxide (self-reduction): $2\mathrm{Cu_2O} + \mathrm{Cu_2S} \rightarrow 6\mathrm{Cu} + \mathrm{SO_2}$.
3. So the reducing agent is copper(I) sulphide, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Thermodynamic Principles of Metallurgy · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 15)

![printed question 45](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q045_b7b1d87d_28102026.png?v=b7b1d87df99a)

## Q46

Biology · botany · Transport in Plants

Root pressure is usually acidic because

- (1) Increase in transpiration
- **(2)** Active absorption
- (3) Low osmotic potential in soil
- (4) Passive absorption

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. As various ions from the soil are actively transported into the vascular tissues of the roots, water follows and increases the pressure inside the xylem i.e., root pressure (positive pressure).

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Root pressure arises because roots actively take up ions into the xylem, so water follows by osmosis and creates a positive pressure. That is active absorption, option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Water Movement up a Plant: Root Pressure and Guttation · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 16)

## Q47

Which one is a wrong statement?

- (1) Brown algae have chlorophyll a and c, and fucoxanthin
- (2) Archegonia are found in Bryophyta, Pteridophyta and Gymnosperms
- **(3)** Mucor has biflagellate zoospores
- (4) Haploid endosperm is typical feature of Gymnosperms

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Mucor has non-motile spore i.e. sporangiospores.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Brown algae contain chlorophyll $a$, $c$ and fucoxanthin, so statement $1$ is correct; archegonia occur in bryophytes, pteridophytes and gymnosperms, so statement $2$ is correct; gymnosperm endosperm is the haploid female gametophyte, so statement $4$ is correct; Mucor forms non-motile sporangiospores, not biflagellate zoospores, so statement $3$ is the wrong one.

## Q49

Biology · zoology · Body Fluids and Circulation

Which one of the following animals has two separate circulatory pathways?

- (1) Shark
- (2) Frog
- (3) Lizard
- **(4)** Whale

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Whale is a mammal which has $4$ chambered heart, so has complete separation of oxygenated and deoxygenated blood.
2. Whale have double circulatory pathways: Systemic and pulmonary circulation.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Two separate circulatory pathways (pulmonary and systemic) means complete double circulation with a four-chambered heart, found in birds and mammals; shark (fish) has single circulation, frog and lizard have incomplete double circulation, and the whale is a mammal, so the answer is option $4$.

## Q50

Biology · botany · Ecosystem

> ⚠ The independent solve chose (1); the booklet prints (3).

Most animals that live in deep oceanic waters are :

- (1) Detritivores
- (2) Primary consumers
- **(3)** Secondary consumers
- (4) Tertiary consumers

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (1) · differs

**Publisher's solution**

1. Detritivores are an important aspect of many ecosystem.
2. They can live on any soil with organic component, including marine ecosystem.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. No sunlight reaches deep oceanic waters, so there is no photosynthesis and no producer base at that depth.
2. Food reaching the deep is mainly dead organic matter (detritus) sinking from the upper layers.
3. So most deep-sea animals feed on detritus, which makes them detritivores, option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Organisms and Trophic Levels · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 16)

![printed question 50](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q050_d9ab17b5_28102026.png?v=d9ab17b58590)

## Q51

Biology · botany · Organisms and Populations

An association of individuals of different species living in the same habitat and having functional interactions is :

- (1) Population
- (2) Ecological niche
- **(3)** Biotic community
- (4) Ecosystem

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Populations of different species occurring in a habitat comprise the biotic community

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A population has one species, a niche is the role of one species, and an ecosystem also includes abiotic components. So an interacting group of different species in one habitat is a $\text{biotic community}$, which is option $3$.

## Q52

Biology · botany · Mineral Nutrition

The oxygen evolved during photosynthesis comes from water molecules. Which one of the following pairs of elements is involved in this reaction?

- (1) Magnesium and Chlorine
- **(2)** Manganese and Chlorine
- (3) Manganese and Potassium
- (4) Magnesium and Molybdenum

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Manganese, chlorine and calcium help in photolysis of water during light reaction of photosynthesis.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Photolysis of water $2H_2O \rightarrow 4H^+ + O_2 + 4e^-$ takes place at the water-splitting complex of PS II, and it needs $Mn^{2+}$ and $Cl^-$ (with $Ca^{2+}$), so the pair is manganese and chlorine, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Essential Mineral Elements: Macronutrients and Micronutrients · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 17)

Sedimentary nutrient cycle – Sulphur and Phosphorus
- (3) Gaseous nutrient cycle – Carbon and sulphur
Sedimentary nutrient cycle – Nitrogen and phosphorus
- (4) Gaseous nutrient cycle – Nitrogen and sulphur

## Q55

Biology · zoology · Neural Control and Coordination

In mammalian eye, the ‘fovea’ is the center of the visual field, where :

- (1) More rods than cones are found.
- **(2)** High density of cones occur, but has no rods
- (3) The optic nerve leaves the eye
- (4) Only rods are present

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Fovea has highest visual acuity which has only cone cells and no rod cells.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The fovea is a thinned-out portion of the retina where only cones are densely packed and rods are absent, so it gives the sharpest vision. The optic nerve leaves at the blind spot, not the fovea. This makes option $2$ correct.

**Subtopic:** Sense Organs: The Eye · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

## Q59

Biology · both · Principles of Inheritance and Variation

> ⚠ The independent solve chose (1); the booklet prints (2).

A colour blind man marries a woman with normal sight who has no history of colour blindness in her family. What is the probability of their grandson being colour blind?

- (1) 0.25
- **(2)** 0.5
- (3) 1
- (4) Nil

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (1) · differs

**Publisher's solution**

1. Father (Colourblind)
2. $\downarrow$ Daughter (Carrier)
3. $\downarrow$ Grandson [$50\%$ Probability ($0.5$)]

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Colour blindness is X-linked recessive: man is $X^cY$, woman with no family history is $XX$.
2. Their children: daughters $X^cX$ (carriers), sons $XY$ (normal).
3. A grandson through a carrier daughter (married to a normal man $XY$) is colour blind with probability $\frac{1}{2}$.
4. A grandson through a normal son receives no $X^c$, so probability $0$.
5. Taking either line equally likely: $\frac{1}{2}\times\frac{1}{2} + \frac{1}{2}\times 0 = 0.25$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Colour Blindness · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 19)

![printed question 59](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q059_3345be0b_28102026.png?v=3345be0bb536)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 59](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q059_de1152de_28102026.png?v=de1152de5dc8)

## Q64

Biology · both · Biomolecules

Which of the following biomolecules does have a phosphodiester bond?

- **(1)** Nucleic acids in a nucleotide
- (2) Fatty acids in a diglyceride
- (3) Monosaccharides in a polysaccharide
- (4) Amino acids in a polypeptide

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Phosphodiester bond is formed between two nucleotides of nucleic acid.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In a nucleic acid, the $3'$-OH of one nucleotide's sugar and the $5'$-OH of the next are linked through a phosphate, forming a $3' \to 5'$ phosphodiester bond.
2. Fatty acids in a glyceride are linked to glycerol by ester bonds, monosaccharides in a polysaccharide by glycosidic bonds, and amino acids in a polypeptide by peptide bonds, so options $2$, $3$, $4$ are out.
3. Only option $1$ involves phosphodiester bonds.

**Subtopic:** Nucleic Acid · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

![printed question 64](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q064_166c9554_28102026.png?v=166c9554f6a9)

## Q65

Biology · zoology · Digestion and Absorption

The primary dentition in human differs from permanent dentition in not having one of the following type of teeth:

- (1) Incisors
- (2) Canine
- **(3)** Premolars
- (4) Molars

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Dental formula of human adult (permanent dentition) $= \frac{2123}{2123}$.
2. Dental formula of child (primary dentition) $= \frac{2102}{2102}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Primary (milk) dentition formula per half jaw: $\frac{2102}{2102}$, i.e. $I=2, C=1, PM=0, M=2$, total $2\times(5+5)=20$ teeth.
2. Permanent dentition formula per half jaw: $\frac{2123}{2123}$, i.e. $I=2, C=1, PM=2, M=3$, total $32$ teeth.
3. Comparing, $PM=0$ in primary dentition, so premolars are the type absent, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Human Digestive System: Alimentary Canal and Associated Glands · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 20)

## Q69

Male gametophyte in angiosperms produces:

- **(2)** Two sperms and a vegetative cell
- (3) Single sperm and a vegetative cell
- (4) Single sperm and two vegetative cells

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. In angiosperms, pollen grain is first male gametophyte.
2. Pollen grain divides into generative cell and vegetative cell.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The male gametophyte of angiosperms (pollen grain) has a vegetative cell and a generative cell; the generative cell divides mitotically to give two male gametes, so it produces two sperms and a vegetative cell, option $2$.

## Q72

- (1) Presence of a segmented body and parthenogenetic mode of reproduction
- (2) Presence of different morphic forms
- **(3)** Alternation of generation between asexual and sexual phases of an organism
- (4) Occurrence of a drastic change in form during post-embryonic development

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. In coelenterates, metagenesis is alternation of generation between polyp and medusa.
2. Polyp reproduces asexually by budding to form medusa and medusa reproduces sexually to form polyp.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Metagenesis is the alternation of an asexual polyp phase with a sexual medusa phase in cnidarians such as $Obelia$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Phylum – Coelenterata (Cnidaria) · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

## Q73

## Q75

Biology · zoology · Structural Organisation in Animals

The function of the gap junction is to :

- (1) Stop substance from leaking across a tissue
- (2) Performing cementing to keep neighbouring cells together
- **(3)** Facilitate communication between adjoining cells by connecting the cytoplasm for rapid transfer of ions, small molecules and some large molecules
- (4) Separate two cells from each other.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Tight junctions stop substances leaking across a tissue and adhering junctions cement neighbouring cells together, so options $1$ and $2$ describe other junctions; gap junctions connect the cytoplasm of adjoining cells for rapid transfer of ions, small molecules and some large molecules, which is option $3$.
