## Q165

Physics · Thermodynamics

The coefficient of performance of a refrigerator is 5. If the temperature inside freezer is $-20^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$, the temperature of the surrounding to which is rejects heat is:

- (1) $21^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$
- **(2)** $31^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$
- (3) $41^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$
- (4) $11^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Refrigerator: heat q2 taken from cold reservoir TC, work input, heat q1 rejected to hot reservoir TH](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q165_sol_1_c535fab7_28102026.png?v=c535fab76862)

*Refrigerator: heat q2 taken from cold reservoir TC, work input, heat q1 rejected to hot reservoir TH*

1. $\mathrm{cop} = \frac{q_1}{w} = \frac{q_2}{q_1 - q_2} = \frac{T_c}{T_H - T_C} = 5$
2. $T_C = 5T_H - 5T_c$
3. $6T_c = 5T_H$
4. $T_H = \frac{6}{5} \times 253\,\mathrm{k} = 303.6\,\mathrm{k} = 30.6^{\circ}\mathrm{C} = 31^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Convert the freezer temperature to kelvin: $T_L = -20 + 273 = 253\,\mathrm{K}$.
2. For a refrigerator, $\beta = \frac{T_L}{T_H - T_L} = 5$.
3. $T_H - T_L = \frac{253}{5} = 50.6\,\mathrm{K}$.
4. $T_H = 253 + 50.6 = 303.6\,\mathrm{K}$.
5. $303.6 - 273 = 30.6^{\circ}\mathrm{C} \approx 31^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Refrigerator and Heat Pump · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 49)

![printed question 165](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q165_0e77d694_28102026.png?v=0e77d694077f)

![printed solution 165](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q165_7a4a8923_28102026.png?v=7a4a8923f356)

## Q166

Physics · Thermodynamics

An ideal gas is compressed to half its initial volume by means of several processes. Which of the process results in the maximum work done on the gas?

- (1) Isothermal
- **(2)** Adiabatic
- (3) Isobaric
- (4) Isochoric

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: P-V diagram: adiabatic, isothermal and isobaric compressions to half volume](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q166_sol_1_71d8d70a_28102026.png?v=71d8d70a1884)

*P-V diagram: adiabatic, isothermal and isobaric compressions to half volume*

1. Since area under the curve is max for adiabatic process so work done on the gas will be max for adiabatic process.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Work done on the gas is $W_{on} = -\int_{V_0}^{V_0/2} P\,dV$, the area under the $P$-$V$ curve between $V_0/2$ and $V_0$.
2. Isochoric: $dV = 0$, so $W = 0$; the volume cannot even halve.
3. Isobaric: $P = P_0$ throughout, so $W = P_0 \frac{V_0}{2}$.
4. Isothermal: $P = P_0 \frac{V_0}{V}$, so $W = P_0 V_0 \ln 2 \approx 0.69\,P_0 V_0 > 0.5\,P_0 V_0$.
5. Adiabatic: $P = P_0 \left(\frac{V_0}{V}\right)^{\gamma}$ with $\gamma > 1$, which lies above the isothermal curve for $V < V_0$, so its area is the largest.
6. Hence $W_{adiabatic} > W_{isothermal} > W_{isobaric} > W_{isochoric}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Adiabatic Process, Thermodynamic Processes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 50)

## Q167

Physics · Work, Energy and Power

A ball is thrown vertically downwards from a height of $20\,\mathrm{m}$ with an initial velocity $v_0$. It collides with the ground, loses 50 percent of its energy in collision and rebounds to the same height. The initial velocity $v_0$ is: (Take $g = 10\,\mathrm{ms^{-2}}$)

- (1) $10\,ms^{-1}$
- (2) $14\,ms^{-1}$
- **(3)** $20\,ms^{-1}$
- (4) $28\,ms^{-1}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: A ball M falling from height h onto the ground, shown at the top and just above the ground](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q167_sol_1_19a21f98_28102026.png?v=19a21f98ff28)

*A ball M falling from height h onto the ground, shown at the top and just above the ground*

1. $\frac{KE_{f}}{KE_{i}} = \frac{1}{2}$
2. $\frac{V_{f}}{V_{i}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$
3. $\frac{\sqrt{2gh}}{\sqrt{V_{0}^{2} + 2gh}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$
4. $V_{0} = 20\,\mathrm{m/sec}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. To rise back to $h = 20\,\mathrm{m}$ the ball needs kinetic energy $\frac{1}{2}mv'^2 = mgh = m(10)(20) = 200m$ just after the collision.
2. It lost $50\%$ of its energy, so just before impact $\frac{1}{2}mv^2 = 2 \times 200m = 400m$, giving $v^2 = 800\,\mathrm{m^2s^{-2}}$.
3. For the downward fall, $v^2 = v_0^2 + 2gh = v_0^2 + 2(10)(20) = v_0^2 + 400$.
4. $v_0^2 = 800 - 400 = 400$, so $v_0 = 20\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Energy Conservation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 50)

![printed question 167](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q167_d361100e_28102026.png?v=d361100e6b40)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 167](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q167_41ba1651_28102026.png?v=41ba16515803)

## Q168

Physics · Work, Energy and Power

On a frictionless surfaces, a block of mass $M$ moving at speed $v$ collides elastically with another block of same mass $M$ which is initially at rest. After collision the first block moves at an angle $\theta$ to its initial direction and has a speed $\frac{v}{3}$. The second block’s speed after the collision is:

- (1) $\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}v$
- **(2)** $\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}v$
- (3) $\frac{3}{4}v$
- (4) $\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}v$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Block M moving at V hits block M at rest; afterwards the two blocks move off at 90 degrees, one at V/3](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q168_sol_1_f193c480_28102026.png?v=f193c4804034)

*Block M moving at V hits block M at rest; afterwards the two blocks move off at 90 degrees, one at V/3*

1. $\vec{P_i} = \vec{P_f}$
2. $\Rightarrow |P_i| = |P_f| \Rightarrow \sqrt{\left(m\frac{V}{3}\right)^{2} + (mV_2)^{2}}$
3. $V_2 = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}V$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The collision is elastic, so kinetic energy is conserved: $\frac{1}{2}Mv^2 = \frac{1}{2}M\left(\frac{v}{3}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{2}Mv_2^2$.
2. Cancel $\frac{1}{2}M$: $v^2 = \frac{v^2}{9} + v_2^2$.
3. $v_2^2 = v^2 - \frac{v^2}{9} = \frac{8v^2}{9}$.
4. $v_2 = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}v$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Problems on Elastic Collision · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 51)

![printed question 168](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q168_1075b4f8_28102026.png?v=1075b4f8fdce)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 168](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q168_5648e35d_28102026.png?v=5648e35d6b4b)

## Q169

Physics · Electrostatics

If potential (in volts) in a region is expressed as $V(x, y, z) = 6xy - y + 2yz$, the electric field (in N/C) at point $(1, 1, 0)$ is:

- (1) $-(6\hat{i} + 9\hat{j} + \hat{k})$
- (2) $-(3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 3\hat{k})$
- **(3)** $-(6\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$
- (4) $-(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $V = 6xy - y + 24z$
2. $\bar{E} = \left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial y}\hat{j}\,\frac{\partial V}{\partial z}\hat{k}\right)$
3. $\bar{E} = \left[(6y)\hat{i} + (6x - 1 + 2z)\hat{j} + (2y)\hat{k}\right]$
4. $\bar{E} = -\left(6\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}\right)$
5. $(1, 1, 0)$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The field is the negative gradient of the potential: $\vec{E} = -\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial y}\hat{j} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial z}\hat{k}\right)$.
2. $\frac{\partial V}{\partial x} = 6y = 6$ at $(1,1,0)$.
3. $\frac{\partial V}{\partial y} = 6x - 1 + 2z = 6 - 1 + 0 = 5$ at $(1,1,0)$.
4. $\frac{\partial V}{\partial z} = 2y = 2$ at $(1,1,0)$.
5. $\vec{E} = -(6\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})\,\text{N/C}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Relation Between Field and Potential · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 51)

![printed question 169](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q169_f76b7e7b_28102026.png?v=f76b7e7b5b4d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 169](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q169_99b0199f_28102026.png?v=99b0199f3391)

## Q170

Physics · Wave Optics

Two slits in Young’s experiment have widths in the ratio $1:25$. The ratio of intensity at the maxima and minima in the interference pattern, $\frac{I_{\mathrm{max}}}{I_{\mathrm{min}}}$ is:

- (1) $\frac{4}{9}$
- **(2)** $\frac{9}{4}$
- (3) $\frac{121}{49}$
- (4) $\frac{49}{121}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\frac{\mathrm{I}_1}{\mathrm{I}_2} = \frac{25}{1} \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{A}_1}{\mathrm{A}_2} = \frac{5}{1}$
2. $\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{max}}}{\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{min}}} = \frac{5 + 1}{5 - 1} = \frac{6}{4} = \frac{3}{2}$
3. $\frac{\mathrm{I}_{\mathrm{max}}}{\mathrm{I}_{\mathrm{min}}} = \left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2} = \frac{9}{4}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Intensity from a slit is proportional to its width, so $\frac{I_1}{I_2} = \frac{1}{25}$.
2. Amplitude goes as the square root of intensity, so $\frac{a_1}{a_2} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{25}} = \frac{1}{5}$.
3. $\frac{I_{\mathrm{max}}}{I_{\mathrm{min}}} = \frac{(a_2 + a_1)^2}{(a_2 - a_1)^2} = \frac{(5+1)^2}{(5-1)^2}$.
4. $\frac{36}{16} = \frac{9}{4}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Intensity Variation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 52)

![printed question 170](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q170_4944c080_28102026.png?v=4944c08016fd)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 170](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q170_543415e1_28102026.png?v=543415e19f8f)

## Q171

Physics · Work, Energy and Power

The heart of a man pumps $5$ litres of blood through the arteries per minute at a pressure of $150\,\mathrm{mm}$ of mercury. If the density of mercury be $13.6 \times 10^{3}\,\mathrm{kg/m^{3}}$ and $g = 10\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$ then the power of heart in watt is:

- (1) $1.50$
- **(2)** $1.70$
- (3) $2.35$
- (4) $3.0$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Power $= \vec{F}\,.\,\vec{V} = P A \vec{V} = \rho g h\, A V$
2. $= 13.6 \times 10^{3} \times 10 \times 150 \times 10^{-3} \times 0.5 \times 10^{-3} / 60$ watt
3. $= \frac{102}{60}$ watt $= 1.70$ watt.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Pressure of $150\,\mathrm{mm}$ of mercury: $p = h\rho g = 0.150 \times 13.6\times10^{3} \times 10 = 2.04\times10^{4}\,\mathrm{Pa}$.
2. Volume flow rate: $Q = \frac{5\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m^3}}{60\,\mathrm{s}} = 8.33\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3/s}$.
3. Power of heart: $P = pQ = 2.04\times10^{4} \times 8.33\times10^{-5} = 1.70\,\mathrm{W}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Power · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 52)

![printed question 171](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q171_6036b99d_28102026.png?v=6036b99db428)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 171](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q171_6c93263b_28102026.png?v=6c93263b4c11)

## Q172

Physics · Magnetic Effect of Electric Current

A proton and an alpha particle both enter a region of uniform magnetic field $B$, moving at right angles to the field $B$. if the radius of circular orbits for both the particles is equal and the kinetic energy acquired by proton is $1\,\mathrm{MeV}$, the energy acquired by the alpha particle will be:

- **(1)** $1\,\mathrm{MeV}$
- (2) $4\,\mathrm{MeV}$
- (3) $0.5\,\mathrm{MeV}$
- (4) $1.5\,\mathrm{MeV}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{R} = \frac{\mathrm{mV}}{\mathrm{qB}} = \frac{\sqrt{2\mathrm{m}(\mathrm{kE})}}{\mathrm{qB}}$
2. Since $\mathrm{R}$ is same so $\mathrm{KE} \propto \frac{\mathrm{q}^{2}}{\mathrm{m}}$
3. So $\mathrm{KE}$ of $\alpha$ particle will be $\frac{(2)^{2}}{4} = \text{same} = 1\,\mathrm{MeV}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For circular motion in the field, $qvB = \frac{mv^2}{r}$, so $r = \frac{mv}{qB} = \frac{\sqrt{2mK}}{qB}$.
2. Rearranging, $K = \frac{q^2 B^2 r^2}{2m}$; with equal $B$ and $r$, $K \propto \frac{q^2}{m}$.
3. For the alpha particle $q_\alpha = 2e$ and $m_\alpha = 4m_p$, so $\frac{K_\alpha}{K_p} = \left(\frac{2e}{e}\right)^2 \frac{m_p}{4m_p} = 4 \times \frac{1}{4} = 1$.
4. $K_\alpha = 1 \times 1\,\mathrm{MeV} = 1\,\mathrm{MeV}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Motion in a Magnetic Field · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 52)

![printed question 172](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q172_8bb65f38_28102026.png?v=8bb65f3881f4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 172](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q172_09fbcfd4_28102026.png?v=09fbcfd4f709)

## Q173

Physics · Semiconductor Electronics

The input signal given to a CE amplifier having a voltage gain of 150 is $V_i = 2\cos\left(15t + \frac{\pi}{3}\right)$. The corresponding output signal will be:

- **(1)** $300\cos\left(15t + \frac{4\pi}{3}\right)$
- (2) $300\cos\left(15t + \frac{\pi}{3}\right)$
- (3) $75\cos\left(15t + \frac{2\pi}{3}\right)$
- (4) $2\cos\left(15t + \frac{5\pi}{6}\right)$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. CE amplifier causes phase difference of $\pi\,(= 180^{\circ})$ so $V_{\mathrm{out}} = 300\cos\left(15t + \frac{\pi}{3} + \pi\right)$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The output amplitude is the gain times the input amplitude: $A_v V_0 = 150 \times 2 = 300$.
2. A CE amplifier gives a phase shift of $\pi$ between input and output, so the output phase is $\frac{\pi}{3} + \pi = \frac{4\pi}{3}$.
3. $V_o = 300\cos\left(15t + \frac{4\pi}{3}\right)$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Transistor as an Amplifier (Common Emitter Configuration): Voltage, Current and Power Gain · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 53)

![printed question 173](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q173_75980fe9_28102026.png?v=75980fe944d6)

## Q174

Physics · Units and Measurements

If dimensions of critical velocity $v_c$ of a liquid flowing through a tube are expressed as $[\eta^{x}\rho^{y}r^{z}]$, where $\eta$, $\rho$ and $r$ are the coefficient of viscosity of liquid, density of liquid and radius of the tube respectively, then the values of $x$, $y$ and $z$ are given by:

- (1) $1, 1, 1$
- **(2)** $1, -1, -1$
- (3) $-1, -1, 1$
- (4) $-1, -1, -1$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $V_c = \eta^{x}\rho^{y}r^{z}$
2. Critical velocity is given by $V_c = \frac{R\eta}{2\rho r}$
3. So, $x = 1$
4. $y = -1$
5. $z = -1$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Write dimensions: $[\eta] = [ML^{-1}T^{-1}]$, $[\rho] = [ML^{-3}]$, $[r] = [L]$, $[v_c] = [LT^{-1}]$.
2. $[LT^{-1}] = [M^{x+y} L^{-x-3y+z} T^{-x}]$.
3. Compare $T$: $-x = -1 \Rightarrow x = 1$.
4. Compare $M$: $x + y = 0 \Rightarrow y = -1$.
5. Compare $L$: $-x - 3y + z = 1 \Rightarrow -1 + 3 + z = 1 \Rightarrow z = -1$.
6. $x, y, z = 1, -1, -1$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Deducing Relation Among the Physical Quantities Using Dimensional Equation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 53)

![printed question 174](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q174_d7500935_28102026.png?v=d7500935d166)

## Q175

Physics · Current Electricity

A circuit contains an ammeter, a battery of $30\,\mathrm{v}$ and a resistance $40.8\,\mathrm{ohm}$ all connected in series. If the ammeter has coil of resistance $480\,\mathrm{ohm}$ and a shunt of $20\,\mathrm{ohm}$, the reading in the ammeter will be:

- (1) $1\,\mathrm{A}$
- **(2)** $0.5\,\mathrm{A}$
- (3) $0.25\,\mathrm{A}$
- (4) $2\,\mathrm{A}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Ammeter (coil S of 480 ohm with a 20 ohm shunt) in series with a 40.8 ohm resistor and a 30 v battery](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q175_sol_1_b5bd79e4_28102026.png?v=b5bd79e47635)

*Ammeter (coil S of 480 ohm with a 20 ohm shunt) in series with a 40.8 ohm resistor and a 30 v battery*

1. Resistance of ammeter $= \frac{480 \times 20}{480 + 20} = 19.2\,\Omega$
2. $i = \frac{30}{40.8 + 19.2} = 0.5\,\mathrm{A}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The coil and shunt are in parallel: $R_A = \frac{480 \times 20}{480 + 20} = \frac{9600}{500} = 19.2\,\Omega$.
2. Total series resistance: $R = 40.8 + 19.2 = 60\,\Omega$.
3. Current read by the ammeter: $I = \frac{V}{R} = \frac{30}{60} = 0.5\,\mathrm{A}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Ammeter and Voltmeter in Circuits · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 53)

![printed question 175](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q175_bf5694eb_28102026.png?v=bf5694eb10c4)

## Q176

Physics · Mechanical Properties of Fluids

Water rises to a height ‘$h$’ in capillary tube. If the length of capillary tube above the surface of water is made less than ‘$h$’, then:

- (1) Water does not rise at all
- (2) Water rises upto the tip of capillary tube and then starts overflowing like a fountain
- **(3)** Water rises upto the top of capillary tube and stays there without overflowing
- (4) Water rises upto a point a little below the top and stays there

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Water will not overflow but will change its radius of curvature.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Capillary rise obeys $h = \frac{2T\cos\theta}{r\rho g}$, i.e. $hR = \frac{2T}{\rho g}$ is constant, where $R = \frac{r}{\cos\theta}$ is the meniscus radius.
2. If the tube length $l < h$, water rises to the top and the meniscus radius increases to $R' = \frac{hR}{l}$ so that $lR' = hR$.
3. The meniscus flattens and the water stays at the top without overflowing (no perpetual fountain), which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Capillary Rise Problems · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 54)

![printed question 176](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q176_c72aec8f_28102026.png?v=c72aec8f9644)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 176](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q176_8b190695_28102026.png?v=8b19069556f7)

## Q177

Physics · Ray Optics

In an astronomical telescope in normal adjustment a straight black line of the length $L$ is drawn on inside part of objective lens. The eye-piece forms a real image of this line. The length of this image is $I$. The magnification of the telescope is:

- **(1)** $\frac{L}{I}$
- (2) $\frac{\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{I}}+1$
- (3) $\frac{\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{I}}-1$
- (4) $\frac{\mathrm{L}+\mathrm{I}}{\mathrm{L}-\mathrm{I}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Objective lens with a line L drawn on it and the eye-piece a distance d = f0 + fe away](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q177_sol_1_10bb1aa0_28102026.png?v=10bb1aa0cc88)

*Objective lens with a line L drawn on it and the eye-piece a distance d = f0 + fe away*

1. Magnification by eyepiece
2. $m = \frac{f}{f+u}$
3. $-\frac{I}{L} = \frac{f_e}{f_e + (-(f_0 + f_e)}$
4. $\Rightarrow \frac{I}{L} = \frac{f_e}{f_0}$
5. $\mathrm{m.\,p.} = \frac{f_0}{f_e} = \frac{L}{I}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In normal adjustment the objective-eyepiece separation is $f_o + f_e$, so the line is at $u = -(f_o + f_e)$ from the eyepiece.
2. Lens formula: $\frac{1}{v} - \frac{1}{u} = \frac{1}{f_e} \Rightarrow \frac{1}{v} = \frac{1}{f_e} - \frac{1}{f_o + f_e} = \frac{f_o}{f_e (f_o + f_e)}$.
3. So $v = \frac{f_e (f_o + f_e)}{f_o}$, a real image.
4. Linear magnification of this image: $\frac{I}{L} = \frac{v}{|u|} = \frac{f_e}{f_o}$.
5. Telescope magnification $M = \frac{f_o}{f_e} = \frac{L}{I}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Resolving and Magnifying Power of Telescope · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 54)

![printed question 177](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q177_2796b43c_28102026.png?v=2796b43ceea3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 177](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q177_5cb5c153_28102026.png?v=5cb5c1533fa5)

## Q178

Physics · Thermal Properties of Matter

The value of coefficient of volume expansion of glycerin is $5 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}$. The fractional change in the density of glycerin for a rise of $40^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ in its temperature is:

- (1) 0.010
- (2) 0.015
- **(3)** 0.020
- (4) 0.025

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\rho = \rho_{0}(1 - \gamma\Delta t)$
2. $\frac{\Delta\rho}{\rho_{0}} = \gamma\Delta T = (5 \times 10^{-4})(40) = 0.02$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Mass is constant, so $\rho V$ is constant and $\rho' = \frac{\rho}{1+\gamma\Delta T}$.
2. For small $\gamma\Delta T$, $\rho' \approx \rho(1-\gamma\Delta T)$, so $\left|\frac{\Delta\rho}{\rho}\right| = \gamma\Delta T$.
3. $\gamma\Delta T = 5\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}} \times 40\,\mathrm{K} = 0.020$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Thermal Expansion · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 55)

## Q179

Physics · Modern Physics

A photoelectric surface is illuminated successively by monochromatic light of wavelength $\lambda$ and $\frac{\lambda}{2}$. If the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted photoelectrons in the second case is 3 times that in the first case, the work function of the surface of the material is:
(h = Planck's constant, c= speed of light)

- (1) $\frac{hc}{3\lambda}$
- **(2)** $\frac{hc}{2\lambda}$
- (3) $\frac{hc}{\lambda}$
- (4) $\frac{2hc}{\lambda}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $k_1 = \frac{hc}{\lambda} - \psi$
2. $k_2 = 3k_1 = \frac{2hc}{\lambda} - \psi = \frac{3hc}{\lambda} - 3\psi$
3. So $2\psi = \frac{hc}{\lambda}$
4. So $\psi = \frac{hc}{2\lambda}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. First case: $K_1 = \frac{hc}{\lambda} - \phi$.
2. Second case: $K_2 = \frac{hc}{\lambda/2} - \phi = \frac{2hc}{\lambda} - \phi$.
3. Given $K_2 = 3K_1$: $\frac{2hc}{\lambda} - \phi = \frac{3hc}{\lambda} - 3\phi$.
4. $2\phi = \frac{hc}{\lambda}$, so $\phi = \frac{hc}{2\lambda}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Photoelectric Effect · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

## Q180

Physics · Ray Optics

A beam of light consisting of red, green and blue colours is incident on a right angled prism. The refractive index of the material of the prism for the above red, green and blue wavelengths are 1.39,1.44 and 1.47 respectively. The prism will:

![printed figure: Blue, green and red rays incident normally on face AB of a right-angled prism ABC with 45 degree angle at C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q180_013fe3aa_28102026.png?v=013fe3aa5b0c)

*Blue, green and red rays incident normally on face AB of a right-angled prism ABC with 45 degree angle at C*

- **(1)** Separate the red colour part from the green and blue colours
- (2) Separate the blue colour part from the red and green colours
- (3) Separate all the three colours from one another
- (4) Not separate the three colours at all

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: A horizontal ray incident at i = 45° on the hypotenuse face of a right-angled 45° prism](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q180_sol_1_7d1a96ea_28102026.png?v=7d1a96eaeee3)

*A horizontal ray incident at i = 45° on the hypotenuse face of a right-angled 45° prism*

1. For TIR $I > I_{c}$ so $\mathrm{Sin}\ i > \sin I_{c}$
2. $\mathrm{Sin}\ 45^{\circ} > \frac{1}{\mu} \Rightarrow \mu\sqrt{2} \Rightarrow \mu = 1.414$
3. Since $\mu$ of green and violet are greater than $1.414$ so they will total internal refrected. But red colour will be vetracted.
4. So Ans. is (1)

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The beam is incident normally on face $AB$, so it enters undeviated.
2. From the geometry ($\angle C = 45^\circ$, $\angle B = 90^\circ$), the ray meets face $AC$ at an angle of incidence $i = 45^\circ$.
3. Total internal reflection at $AC$ requires $\sin 45^\circ > \frac{1}{n}$, i.e. $n > \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414$.
4. Red: $n = 1.39 < 1.414$, so red refracts out through $AC$.
5. Green: $n = 1.44 > 1.414$ and blue: $n = 1.47 > 1.414$, so both are totally internally reflected.
6. Hence red is separated from green and blue, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Total Internal Reflection (TIR) · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)
